Let me write a comprehensive summary document of the findings: ## Summary: Triage Reset Mechanism in Desloppify I've conducted a thorough analysis of the triage reset mechanism in the desloppify codebase. Here are my findings: --- ## 1. The 4-Stage Triage Process The triage system implements a **linear 4-stage workflow** defined in `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/plan/triage_playbook.py`: ```python TRIAGE_STAGE_IDS = ( "triage::observe", "triage::reflect", "triage::organize", "triage::commit", ) TRIAGE_STAGE_DEPENDENCIES: dict[str, set[str]] = { "observe": set(), "reflect": {"observe"}, "organize": {"reflect"}, "commit": {"organize"}, } ``` **Workflow sequence:** - **OBSERVE** — Analyze findings, spot contradictions (100 char minimum report) - **REFLECT** — Compare against completed work, detect recurring patterns (200+ char minimum) - **ORGANIZE** — Defer contradictions, cluster findings, prioritize (requires enriched clusters) - **COMMIT** — Write strategy & confirm plan is ready (200 char minimum or "same") --- ## 2. What Triggers Triage Reset (Going Back to "observe") **The key detection mechanism is in** `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_plan/stale_dimensions.py` **in the `sync_triage_needed()` function (lines 363-417).** ### Conditions that trigger reset: 1. **New review findings appear** (not just resolutions) - Calculated by comparing **finding snapshot hash** before and after scan - Uses `review_finding_snapshot_hash(state)` to hash all open review/concerns findings 2. **Hash mismatch detected** AND **new findings exist** ```python current_hash = review_finding_snapshot_hash(state) last_hash = meta.get("finding_snapshot_hash", "") if current_hash and current_hash != last_hash and not already_present: # Check if genuinely NEW findings appeared current_review_ids = {...} # Current open findings triaged_ids = set(meta.get("triaged_ids", [])) new_since_triage = current_review_ids - triaged_ids if new_since_triage: # INJECT ALL 4 TRIAGE STAGES AT FRONT OF QUEUE # Reset back to "observe" stage ``` 3. **Critical distinction:** - **If findings were RESOLVED** (current IDs ⊂ previously triaged IDs): hash updates silently, NO re-triage - **If NEW findings appeared** (current IDs has items not in triaged_ids): ALL 4 stages injected at front ### Where the reset happens: **File:** `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/scan/scan_workflow.py` **Function:** `_reconcile_plan_post_scan()` (line 253) ```python triage_sync = sync_triage_needed(plan, runtime.state) if triage_sync.changes: dirty = True if triage_sync.injected: print("Plan: planning mode needed — review findings changed since last triage.") append_log_entry(plan, "sync_triage", actor="system", detail={"injected": True}) ``` --- ## 3. File Modification Detection The file change detection happens **indirectly through the review findings mechanism**: 1. **Scan generates review findings** that are either new or removed 2. **The plan tracks finding snapshot hash:** ```python def review_finding_snapshot_hash(state: StateModel) -> str: """Hash open review finding IDs to detect changes.""" findings = state.get("findings", {}) review_ids = sorted( fid for fid, f in findings.items() if […]

Here's what I found: ### 1. **CRITICAL: `except Exception` without re-raise - BEST-EFFORT RECOVERY PATTERN** **File:** `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/plan/override_handlers.py:84` ```python try: state_mod.save_state(state_data, effective_state_path) save_plan(plan, effective_plan_path) except Exception: _restore_file_snapshot(effective_state_path, state_snapshot) _restore_file_snapshot(effective_plan_path, plan_snapshot) raise ``` **Assessment:** INTENTIONAL - This is a rollback/transaction pattern. Catches all exceptions to clean up, then re-raises. This is acceptable. **File:** `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/review/batches.py:528` ```python except SystemExit as exc: append_run_log(f"run-finished import-failed code={exc.code}") raise except Exception as exc: append_run_log(f"run-finished import-error error={exc}") raise ``` **Assessment:** INTENTIONAL - Logging then re-raising. This is acceptable. --- ### 2. **SILENT ERROR SWALLOWING - HIGH RISK** **File:** `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/resolve/apply.py:24` ```python try: if not has_living_plan(): return None plan = load_plan() cluster = plan.get("clusters", {}).get(pattern) if cluster and cluster.get("finding_ids"): return list(cluster["finding_ids"]) except (OSError, ValueError, KeyError, TypeError) as exc: _ = exc return None ``` **Assessment:** PROBLEMATIC - Silently swallows all plan-loading errors (file not found, corrupt JSON, missing keys, type mismatches) and returns None. This masks both operational failures (disk errors) and programming bugs (wrong data structure). Best-effort pattern but hides debugging information. **File:** `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/status_cmd.py:151` ```python except (ImportError, OSError, ValueError, KeyError, TypeError): pass ``` **Assessment:** PROBLEMATIC - Bare `pass` with no logging. Silently ignores any error from rendering uncommitted reminder. Mixed programming errors (ValueError, KeyError) with operational errors (OSError, ImportError). **File:** `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/resolve/render.py:234` ```python except (ImportError, OSError, ValueError, KeyError, TypeError): logging.getLogger(__name__).debug( "commit guidance rendering skipped", exc_info=True, ) ``` **Assessment:** ACCEPTABLE - Has logging with `exc_info=True`. Best-effort graceful degradation for optional feature. --- ### 3. **MIXED OPERATIONAL + PROGRAMMING ERRORS - PROBLEMATIC PATTERNS** These catch OSError/IOError (operational) mixed with KeyError/ValueError/TypeError (programming errors): **File:** `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/exclude_cmd.py:87` ```python except (OSError, ValueError, TypeError, KeyError): print(colorize(" Warning: could not update living plan.", "yellow"), file=sys.stderr) ``` **Assessment:** PROBLEMATIC - Mixes operational errors (OSError: file not found) with programming errors (KeyError: missing dict key, TypeError: wrong type). A KeyError likely indicates a bug in how plan.json is structured, not an expected operational failure. **File:** `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/resolve/cmd.py:116` ```python except (OSError, ValueError, KeyError, TypeError): print(colorize(" Warning: could not update living plan.", "yellow"), file=sys.stderr) ``` **Assessment:** PROBLEMATIC - Same issue. KeyError/TypeError should not be mixed with OSError. **File:** `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/resolve/queue_guard.py:94` ```python except (OSError, ValueError, KeyError, TypeError): return False ``` **Assessment:** PROBLEMATIC - Silent swallowing (via return False) of KeyError/TypeError. Let me create a final summary document. ## Exploration Report: `desloppify next` Command Rendering Layer ### Overview The `next` command's rendering layer demonstrates significant **domain leakage** — scoring, policy, and subjective integrity computations are intermingled with presentation-only formatting code. The rendering layer should be pure presentation but instead performs critical scoring and policy decisions. --- ### File Structure **Location:** `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/next_parts/` Files in the rendering layer: 1. **`next.py`** — Main command orchestrator (scoring/policy decisions) 2. **`output.py`** — JSON serialization and file I/O (pure presentation) 3. **`render.py`** — Terminal rendering with embedded domain logic (MIXED) 4. **`render_support.py`** — Shared render helpers with some domain logic (MIXED) --- ### Key Finding: Domain Leakage in `render.py` #### **1. Scoring Impact Computation (Lines 133-189)** **Function:** `_render_score_impact()` ```python def _render_score_impact( item: dict, dim_scores: dict, potentials: dict | None, ) -> None: ``` **Domain Leakage:** - Line 139: `scoring_mod.get_dimension_for_detector(detector)` — looks up scoring metadata - Line 154: `compute_score_impact(dim_scores, potentials, detector, issues_to_fix=1)` — **SCORES COMPUTATION** - This computes the estimated point improvement from fixing one issue - Pure scoring domain logic, not presentation - Lines 162-167: Calls `compute_score_impact()` again for bulk fix estimates - Line 174: `compute_health_breakdown(dim_scores)` — **SCORING AGGREGATION** - Computes weighted health score breakdown - Another pure scoring function in rendering code **What Should Happen:** - Impact estimates should be pre-computed in the data layer (`next.py` or work-queue building) - Renderer should only format pre-computed impact strings, not calculate them #### **2. This is user-facing reporting code. **Required Fix:** Wrap in try-except: ```python try: _plan = load_plan() except PLAN_LOAD_EXCEPTIONS: _plan = {} if _plan.get("queue_order") or _plan.get("clusters"): _plan_data = _plan ``` --- ### CRITICAL FINDING #2: `import_cmd.py` (Review Command) **File:** `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/review/import_cmd.py` **Line 192:** Unguarded `load_plan()` call in `_sync_plan_after_import()` ```python 164 def _sync_plan_after_import(state: dict, diff: dict, assessment_mode: str) -> None: 165 """All post-import plan syncs. It adds real behavior that `core/query.py` does not have: 1. The `QueryWriter` dataclass provides an explicit failure policy, separating `write()` (returns `OutputResult`) from `write_best_effort()` (prints a contextual yellow stderr warning on failure). That failure-logging logic lives only in `helpers/query.py`. 2. `query_writer()` binds to the active runtime query path via `query_file_path()` — a runtime context concern `core/query.py` doesn't handle. 3. `write_query_best_effort()` is a two-step composed call that would need to live somewhere. `core/query.py` is the real logic: payload budget enforcement, truncation, config injection, JSON serialization. `helpers/query.py` is the command-layer adapter — it provides the named-warning pattern and runtime binding. The chain has exactly two levels with distinct responsibilities at each. Not low-leverage. --- ### Finding 2: facade_hub_coupling **Verdict: MOSTLY FALSE POSITIVE — one partial concern** Read all four files: - **`desloppify/state.py`**: Re-exports across 6 internal submodules (`_state.filtering`, `_state.merge`, `_state.noise`, `_state.persistence`, `_state.resolution`, `_state.schema`, `_state.scoring`). It adds one genuine function: `score_snapshot()` which computes a `ScoreSnapshot` NamedTuple from four separate score getters — real composition logic that doesn't exist in any submodule. So: mostly re-export hub, but not pure. - **`desloppify/engine/plan.py`**: Re-exports across 9 internal submodules. Adds one genuine function: `triage_phase_banner()` which checks queue order against `TRIAGE_IDS` and formats a banner string. Not a zero-logic shim. - **`desloppify/core/output_api.py`**: Pure re-export of `COLORS`, `LOC_COMPACT_THRESHOLD`, `NO_COLOR`, `colorize`, `display_entries`, `log`, `print_table` from `core/output.py`. Zero logic added. This is the clearest hub. - **`desloppify/core/discovery_api.py`**: Pure re-export of items from `core/file_paths` and `core/source_discovery`. Zero logic added. The type annotation is **genuinely incorrect** — it should be `state_file: Path | None` instead of `state_file: str`.

The tool never receives untrusted input -- it is invoked directly by the developer on their own codebase. --- ### 2. Every one of these calls uses hardcoded command names (e.g., `["git", "rev-parse", "HEAD"]`, `["npx", "knip", ...]`, `["ruff", ...]`, `["bandit", ...]`). The arguments come from the tool's own scan path (provided by the developer at the CLI) and internal constants -- not from any external/untrusted source. --- ### 3. This is standard practice for CLI tools that rely on the user's PATH. Using absolute paths would be wrong here -- it would break portability across systems. They are not used for any security purpose -- no passwords, no signatures, no authentication tokens. SHA1/MD5 are perfectly appropriate for content-addressable keys. Here is the full analysis: --- ## Deferred Import Analysis: All 10 Files --- ### 1. `app/commands/helpers/queue_progress.py` **Deferred imports** (inside `print_execution_or_reveal` and `show_score_with_plan_context`): ```python # Line 355 — inside print_execution_or_reveal() from desloppify import state as state_mod # Line 363 — inside print_execution_or_reveal() from desloppify.app.commands.helpers.score_update import print_score_update # Line 378 — inside show_score_with_plan_context() from desloppify.engine.plan import load_plan ``` And inside `plan_aware_queue_breakdown()` (lines 58-59): ```python from desloppify.engine._work_queue.plan_order import collapse_clusters from desloppify.engine.work_queue import QueueBuildOptions, build_work_queue ``` **Is there a real cycle?** - `queue_progress.py` already imports `desloppify.engine.work_queue` at module level (line 13: `from desloppify.engine.work_queue import is_subjective_queue_item`). So deferring the `build_work_queue` / `QueueBuildOptions` import from the same module inside `plan_aware_queue_breakdown` is **unnecessary** — those names live in the exact same module that's already imported at the top. - `from desloppify import state as state_mod` and `from desloppify.engine.plan import load_plan` — `queue_progress` does not import either at module level. Naming Conventions **Function verb prefixes — mostly disciplined, but three concrete tensions:** **`get_` vs `load_` for retrieval operations.** The split is mostly principled — `get_` for lightweight accessors, `load_` for I/O — but it breaks down in one place. Both `core/subjective_dimensions.py` and `intelligence/review/dimensions/metadata.py` define identically named functions: ``` # core/subjective_dimensions.py def get_dimension_metadata(...) # accessor def load_subjective_dimension_metadata(...) # loads from YAML # intelligence/review/dimensions/metadata.py def get_dimension_metadata(...) # same accessor, different module def load_subjective_dimension_metadata(...) # same loader, different module ``` These are **mirror implementations** — the same public function names living in two separate packages, which is the symptom of an unfinished extraction rather than intentional naming. **`build_` (104) vs `make_` (53) for construction.** In production code `build_` tends toward complex assembly (building context, packets, graphs) and `make_` toward simpler factory patterns, but there are files where both appear without a discernible distinction: ```python # languages/_framework/__init__.py def make_lang_config(...) # lazy wrapper # engine/concerns.py — uses _build_* for internal assembly # app/commands/review/ — uses _build_* and _make_* interchangeably for packet construction ``` **`show_` (37) vs `print_` (98) for terminal output.** These are not synonyms but the naming doesn't signal the distinction clearly. `print_` always writes to stdout (`-> None`). `show_` is supposed to also print, but three functions in `scan_reporting_dimensions.py` have no return type annotation at all — so the semantic contract isn't enforced: ```python # The relationship is a clean delegation, not a duplication: **`scan_reporting_presentation.py` is the canonical implementation.** It contains all actual logic with dependency-injected parameters (protocol stubs for `state_mod`, `narrative_mod`, `registry_mod`, and `colorize_fn`). This makes it unit-testable in isolation. **`scan_reporting_dimensions.py` is the thin facade.** It imports from `scan_reporting_presentation` and wraps every call by supplying the concrete module dependencies: ```python # scan_reporting_dimensions.py line 7 from . import scan_reporting_presentation as presentation_mod def show_detector_progress(state: dict): return presentation_mod.show_detector_progress( state, state_mod=state_mod, narrative_mod=narrative_mod, registry_mod=registry_mod, colorize_fn=colorize, ) def show_score_model_breakdown(state, *, dim_scores=None): return presentation_mod.show_score_model_breakdown( state, scoring_mod=scoring_mod, colorize_fn=colorize, dim_scores=dim_scores, ) def show_dimension_deltas(prev, current): return presentation_mod.show_dimension_deltas( prev, current, scoring_mod=scoring_mod, colorize_fn=colorize, ) def show_low_dimension_hints(dim_scores): return presentation_mod.show_low_dimension_hints( dim_scores, scoring_mod=scoring_mod, colorize_fn=colorize, ) ``` None of the four functions are duplicated — `scan_reporting_dimensions.py` delegates 100% to `scan_reporting_presentation.py`. The import direction is one-way: `dimensions` imports `presentation`, never the reverse. --- ## 2. Helper naming collision — `helpers.py` vs `shared.py` in `plan/triage/` Both files exist. They serve **different purposes** and should not be merged: **`helpers.py`** — Low-level triage business logic (pure functions): - `_has_triage_in_queue`, `_inject_triage_stages`, `_purge_triage_stage` - `_cascade_clear_later_confirmations`, `_print_cascade_clear_feedback` - `_observe_dimension_breakdown`, `_open_review_ids_from_state` - `_triage_coverage`, `_manual_clusters_with_issues` - `_apply_completion` (the completion-state writer) - `_find_cluster_for`, `_count_log_activity_since` **`shared.py`** — A re-export compatibility shim. It has **no logic of its own** — it only re-exports names from four other submodules: - From `progress_render`: `_print_progress`, `_print_stage_progress` - From `reflect_dashboard`: `_print_reflect_dashboard` - From `stage_helpers`: `_manual_clusters_with_issues`, `_require_triage_pending`, `_triage_coverage`, `_unenriched_clusters`, `_validate_stage_report` - From `stage_persistence`: `record_triage_stage`, `refresh_stage_snapshot` Note that `shared.py` re-exports `_manual_clusters_with_issues` and `_triage_coverage`, which are also defined directly in `helpers.py`. These appear to be maintained in parallel in `stage_helpers.py` as well. `shared.py` is a compatibility surface for callers that import from a single `shared` namespace; merging it into `helpers.py` would break those callers. --- ## 3. `__init__.py` files with `from` imports but no `__all__` The following files (absolute paths) have `from ... import ...` statements but define no `__all__`: | File | What it imports | |---|---| | `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/__init__.py` | `from __future__ import annotations` (stdlib only, `__all__` arguably not needed) | | `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/languages/bash/__init__.py` | `generic_lang`, `BASH_SPEC` | | `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/languages/clojure/__init__.py` | `generic_lang`, `CLOJURE_SPEC` | | `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/languages/csharp/__init__.py` | Large set (20+ names from extractors, phases, review, zones, registry hooks) | | `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/languages/cxx/__init__.py` | `generic_lang`, `CPP_SPEC` | | `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/languages/dart/__init__.py` | Large set (extractors, phases, review, zones, registry hooks) | | `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/languages/elixir/__init__.py` | `generic_lang`, `ELIXIR_SPEC` | | `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/languages/erlang/__init__.py` | […]

**enums.py**: Canonical enums for issue attributes (Confidence, Status, Tier) - **exception_sets.py**: Shared exception types (CommandError, PacketValidationError, TriageValidationError) #### 2. **Base Infrastructure** - **__init__.py**: Package root (intentionally empty re-export hub) - **output_contract.py**: OutputResult dataclass for best-effort side-effect outcomes - **runtime_state.py**: FileTextCache + RuntimeContext for in-memory caches and exclusions - **compatibility.py**: Import-boundary governance matrix (PUBLIC_RUNTIME_ROOTS, SOFT_DEPRECATED_MODULES, PRIVATE_MODULE_PREFIXES) #### 3. **Path & File Management** - **file_paths.py**: Low-level path resolution (matches_exclusion, rel, resolve_path, safe_write_text) - **paths_api.py**: Public path facade (re-exports text_api helpers, PROJECT_ROOT, DEFAULT_PATH, SRC_PATH) - **path_io_api.py**: Public path I/O facade (wraps file_paths) - **discovery_api.py**: Public discovery facade (unifies file_paths + source_discovery exports) - **source_discovery.py**: Source-file discovery, exclusion matching, scan-scoped content caching (enable_file_cache, disable_file_cache, read_file_text) #### 4. **Text & Project Root Utilities** - **_internal/text_utils.py**: Low-level text utilities (get_project_root, read_code_snippet, get_area) - **text/text_api.py**: Public text facade (wraps _internal/text_utils) - **text/__init__.py**: Package root (empty) #### 5. **Coercion Utilities** - **_internal/coercions.py**: Pure input coercers (coerce_positive_int, coerce_positive_float, coerce_confidence) - **coercions_api.py**: Public coercion facade (wraps _internal/coercions) #### 6. **Search & Query** - **grep.py**: Regex-based file searching (grep_files, grep_files_containing) - **search/search_query.py**: Query payload writing (search_query payload generation, truncation, size management) - **search/search_query_paths.py**: Query file path resolution (query_file_path, set_query_file) - **search/__init__.py**: Package root (empty) #### 7. **Configuration** - **config.py**: Project-wide + language-specific config (.desloppify/config.json) loading/persistence #### 8. **Registry & Metadata** - **registry.py**: Canonical detector metadata (DISPLAY_ORDER, FixerConfig, detector definitions) - **signal_patterns.py**: Shared regex policies (DEPRECATION_MARKER_RE, MIGRATION_TODO_RE, SERVICE_ROLE_TOKEN_RE) - **subjective_dimensions.py**: Dimension metadata (DISPLAY_NAMES, load_dimensions) - **skill_docs.py**: Skill-document versioning (SKILL_VERSION, SKILL_VERSION_RE, SKILL_SEARCH_PATHS) #### 9. **I/O & Output** - **output.py**: Terminal output helpers (colorize, LOC formatting, COLORS dict) - **fallbacks.py**: Best-effort fallback helpers (log_best_effort_failure, print_error, warn_best_effort) - **issues_render.py**: Issue rendering helpers (issue_weight, confidence scoring) #### 10. **System Integration** - **git_context.py**: Read-only git observation (detect_git_context, GitContext dataclass) - **tooling.py**: Tool metadata helpers (tool hashing, staleness checks) - **optional_deps.py**: Optional dependency checks (has_module, has_tree_sitter_language_pack) --- ### Internal Dependency Graph ``` [Lowest-level / No internal deps]: ├── enums.py ├── exception_sets.py ├── output_contract.py ├── optional_deps.py ├── signal_patterns.py ├── tooling.py └── git_context.py [_internal layer (private)]: ├── _internal/__init__.py ├── _internal/coercions.py (pure) └── _internal/text_utils.py → runtime_state [Public facades layer]: ├── runtime_state.py (context management, no deps on other core modules) ├── coercions_api.py → _internal/coercions ├── text/text_api.py → _internal/text_utils └── text/__init__.py [Path/file layer]: […]

The Layer Cake: Actual Dependency Map The intended hierarchy, reading the package docstrings, appears to be: ``` app/ (top: CLI, commands, output surfaces) | intelligence/ (mid-high: narrative coaching, review preparation, subjective workflows) | engine/ (mid: detectors, scoring, state, planning, work queue) | core/ (bottom: shared primitives, file discovery, text utils, enums, config) | languages/ (side-car: plugin registry, language-specific logic) ``` ### What the actual import graph says: | Source | Targets (non-test, file count) | |---------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------| | `app/` | core (102), engine (60), intelligence (26), languages (13), state (13) | | `engine/` | core (38), state (7), languages (4), intelligence (4), **app (3)** | | `intelligence/`| core (22), engine (18), state (14), languages (5) | | `languages/` | core (78), engine (51), state (9), hook_registry (8) | | `core/` | state (1), search (1), **engine (1)**, **intelligence (1)**, **languages (1)** | ### Layer violations (real architectural problems): **1a. `core/` imports from `engine/`, `intelligence/`, and `languages/`** These are the specific files: - `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/core/subjective_dimensions.py` imports from `intelligence.review.dimensions.data`, `intelligence.review.dimensions.metadata`, and `languages` (available_langs). This is a 450-line module that aggregates subjective dimension metadata. It does not belong in `core/` -- it bridges intelligence and engine concepts, aggregating dimension metadata from review prompts. It should live in `engine/_scoring/` or `intelligence/` since it depends on both. Similarly, `core/output/issues.py` depends on scoring policy. These upward imports from `core/` violate the fundamental premise that `core/` is the dependency-free foundation. **1b. `engine/` imports from `app/` (3 files)** - `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/planning/dimension_rows.py:34` -- deferred import of `app.output.scorecard_parts.dimensions.prepare_scorecard_dimensions` inside a try/except. The third (`context.py`) is a hard upward dependency: the engine's work queue context depends on an app-layer helper at import time. Now I have a comprehensive picture. Let me write the full analysis. --- # Deep Beauty Review: Patterns and Internal Consistency ## 1. Command Entry Point Pattern **Assessment: Evolving (old pattern migrating to a new, cleaner one)** There is a clear intended pattern: every top-level command is a function with signature `cmd_XXX(args: argparse.Namespace) -> None`. The registry in `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/registry.py` (lines 12-51) imports all these handlers into a flat dict. This is clean and consistent -- every command registered matches the `CommandHandler = Callable[[Any], None]` type alias. However, beneath that uniform surface, there are **two distinct state-access patterns** in active use: **Pattern A (newer, cleaner):** Use `command_runtime(args)` to get a `CommandRuntime` dataclass (config + state + state_path bundled). Used by: `show`, `status`, `next`, `plan`, `exclude`, `detect`, `review`, `config`, `zone`. ```python # From show/cmd.py line 87-89 runtime = command_runtime(args) state = runtime.state config = runtime.config ``` **Pattern B (older):** Manually call `state_path(args)` then `state_mod.load_state(state_file)`. Used by: `resolve`, `scan`, and several `plan/override_handlers` subcommands. ```python # From resolve/cmd.py lines 102-103 state_file = state_path(args) state = state_mod.load_state(state_file) ``` The `resolve` command's bypass is probably intentional -- it needs to write state back via its own `_save_state_or_exit` flow. But `plan/override_handlers.py` has functions that call `command_runtime(args).state` on line 105 and also call `state_mod.load_state(state_file)` manually on lines 193, 308, 334 -- mixing both patterns in the same file. And `scan` has its own bespoke runtime construction in `scan/workflow.py` that builds a large `ScanRuntime` dataclass with scan-specific fields. The `scan` command also stands apart structurally. Every other major command is 50-200 lines of orchestration. The `scan` command at `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/scan/cmd.py` is 219 lines, but it delegates to a `ScanOrchestrator` class -- a pattern no other command uses. **Common boilerplate** in nearly every command handler (show, status, next) follows this sequence: 1. `command_runtime(args)` 2. `require_completed_scan(state)` 3. `check_skill_version()` + `check_config_staleness()` warnings 4. `print_triage_guardrail_info()` This is consistent but not abstracted -- it is copy-pasted across commands. --- ## 2. Language Plugin Pattern **Assessment: Consistent and good, with a clean two-tier design** The language plugin system is one of the strongest architectural patterns in the codebase. There are two tiers: **Full plugins** (Python, TypeScript, Go, C#, Dart, GDScript) follow a highly consistent structure: - `__init__.py`: Class extending `LangConfig`, decorated with `@register_lang("name")` - `commands.py`: `get_detect_commands()` returning a dict - `detectors/`: Language-specific detector implementations - `extractors.py`: Function extraction for duplicate detection - `phases.py`: Phase runner functions with uniform signature - `review.py` + `review_data/`: Subjective review configuration […]

Top-Level Architecture Naming The codebase follows a clean four-layer structure: ``` desloppify/ app/ -- CLI presentation layer (parsers, commands, output) core/ -- Shared infrastructure (discovery, search, output, enums, config) engine/ -- Domain logic (detectors, scoring, state, plan, work queue) intelligence/ -- Higher-order analysis (narrative coaching, review, integrity) languages/ -- Language plugins (_framework + per-language implementations) ``` **Verdict: Looks odd but actually fine.** The word "intelligence" initially feels pretentious for what amounts to "LLM-adjacent analysis," but once you read the contents, it makes sense as a layer distinction. `intelligence/narrative/` computes coaching advice (phase detection, headline generation, strategy). `intelligence/review/` prepares structured review packets for LLM evaluation. The common thread is "computed insight from raw data" -- distinct from `engine/` which is pure mechanical detection. A newcomer might wonder "is this an AI module?", but the docstring immediately clarifies it: "pure Python, no LLM calls happen here." The alternative names (`analysis/`, `insight/`, `synthesis/`) are either too generic or too close to existing names. This is fine. ### 2. The `helpers.py` / `core.py` / `base.py` / `*_support.py` Assessment I found 24 vaguely-named modules. Each module inside has a specific name and a narrow purpose. The directory-level name `helpers/` is the weakest possible name for "cross-cutting command utilities," but the individual modules inside are well-named. **Looks odd but actually fine** -- the alternative would be something like `app/commands/_shared/`, which is marginally better at best. - **`engine/detectors/base.py`** -- Contains `FunctionInfo`, `ClassInfo`, `ComplexitySignal` dataclasses -- shared types for cross-language detector data. The name "base" is accurate: these are base types that detectors build on. **Looks odd but actually fine.** - **`engine/_work_queue/core.py`** -- The main work-queue builder (`build_work_queue`, `group_queue_items`). Inside a `_work_queue/` package, `core.py` for the primary entry point is standard Python convention. **Looks odd but actually fine.** - **`engine/_scoring/policy/core.py`**, **`engine/_scoring/results/core.py`**, **`engine/_scoring/subjective/core.py`** -- Each is the central module of its sub-package. Standard Python pattern. **Fine.** - **`intelligence/narrative/core.py`** -- Contains `compute_narrative()`, the orchestrator. **Fine.** #### Vague names that are mildly suboptimal: - **`app/commands/review/helpers.py`** -- Contains a single 6-line function `parse_dimensions()`. The `core/output/` vs `app/output/` Split Both `core/output/` and `app/output/` exist: - `core/output/` -- Low-level terminal primitives (`colorize`, `log_best_effort_failure`, `print_error`, `OutputResult`, `issue_weight`) - `app/output/` -- High-level visual output (`scorecard`, `scorecard_parts/`, `tree_text`, `visualize`) **Verdict: Looks odd but actually fine.** This follows the same layering as the rest of the codebase: `core/` = shared primitives, `app/` = presentation logic. The `core/output/terminal.py` module is the second-most imported module in the codebase (109 imports). Having it in `core/` rather than `app/` is correct because engine-layer code also uses `colorize`. ### 7. Detector/Fixer/Phase Naming Conventions Detector names in the registry (`core/registry.py`) use snake_case nouns or noun phrases: `unused`, `logs`, `exports`, `structural`, `orphaned`, `uncalled_functions`, `flat_dirs`, `single_use`, `coupling`, `cycles`, `facade`, `dupes`, `naming`, `smells`, `react`, `props`, `dict_keys`, `test_coverage`, `security`, `review`, `subjective_review`, `concerns`, `global_mutable_config`, `private_imports`, `layer_violation`, `responsibility_cohesion`, `boilerplate_duplication`, `stale_wontfix`, `stale_exclude`, `signature`, `deprecated`, `patterns`. **Minor inconsistencies:** - Most names are bare nouns (`unused`, `logs`, `dupes`, `naming`), but a few use compound descriptors (`global_mutable_config`, `responsibility_cohesion`, `boilerplate_duplication`, `layer_violation`, `private_imports`, `uncalled_functions`). The longer names tend to be newer detectors that needed disambiguation. - `stale_wontfix` and `stale_exclude` share the `stale_` prefix, but no other detectors use a `stale_` pattern. **Verdict: Looks odd but actually fine.** The naming is pragmatic. Short names for common detectors (`unused`, `smells`, `dupes`), longer descriptive names for specialized ones. The inconsistency in length is actually useful: it creates a natural "importance hierarchy" where the high-frequency detectors have memorable one-word names. Language plugin structure is very consistent across all 6 full implementations (python, typescript, csharp, dart, go, gdscript): - All have: `__init__.py`, `commands.py`, `detectors/`, `extractors.py`, `phases.py`, `review.py`, `review_data/` - Languages with fixers have: `fixers/` - Languages with move support have: `move.py` Python has some extra split files (`extractors_classes.py`, `extractors_shared.py`, `phases_quality.py`, `phases_runtime.py`) that other languages do not have. This reflects Python being the most mature plugin with more complexity, not an inconsistency. ### 8. The `runner_*` Naming Pattern In `app/commands/review/`, there are four `runner_*.py` files: - `runner_parallel.py` -- Thread pool execution for review batches - `runner_process.py` -- Subprocess management for review batch execution - `runner_packets.py` -- Packet sanitization and artifact layout - `runner_failures.py` -- Failure classification and reporting These share the `runner_` prefix as a cohesion signal: they are all parts of the review batch execution infrastructure. The alternative would be a `runner/` sub-package. The `runner_packets.py` name is slightly misleading […]

Let me compile the comprehensive investigation report: ## Code Quality Investigation Report: Desloppify Codebase Based on thorough analysis of the desloppify codebase, here are my findings on the five code quality issues: --- ### 1. **Error Handling Inconsistency: `raise SystemExit` vs Domain-Specific Exceptions** **Findings:** I found **10 instances of `raise SystemExit`** across the main codebase (excluding test and worktree directories): - `/desloppify/app/output/visualize.py` (1 instance) - `/desloppify/app/commands/show/cmd.py` (1 instance) - `/desloppify/app/commands/scan/scan.py` (2 instances) - `/desloppify/app/commands/review/batches.py` (2 instances: line 709, 877) - `/desloppify/app/commands/review/external.py` (1 instance) - `/desloppify/app/commands/dev_cmd.py` (2 instances) - `/desloppify/app/commands/next.py` (1 instance) **Analysis of `raise SystemExit` Justification:** - **batches.py:709** (`raise SystemExit(130) from None`): **JUSTIFIED**. This is a KeyboardInterrupt handler catching Ctrl+C, properly using exit code 130 (standard Unix signal exit convention). This is appropriate early-exit behavior. - **batches.py:877** (`raise SystemExit(followup_code)`): **QUESTIONABLE**. This propagates an exit code from a followup scan. Could be a normal flow error better handled with domain exceptions. - **dev_cmd.py**: Uses `raise SystemExit(colorize(...))` with a **string argument** — this is **BROKEN**. SystemExit expects an int exit code, not a message. Should use logging/error printing instead. - **show/cmd.py, scan/scan.py, next.py, visualize.py, external.py**: These need individual review, but the pattern suggests they may be handling truly critical/early-exit scenarios (missing required files, configuration failures). **Missing Domain-Specific Exception:** I found no `PlanError` or `CommandError` class in the codebase. There is: - `LangResolutionError(SystemExit)` in `/desloppify/app/commands/helpers/lang.py` - `LangRuntimeOptionsError(ValueError)` in `/desloppify/app/commands/helpers/runtime_options.py` **Plan Loading Strategy:** The plan loading does NOT raise exceptions broadly. Instead, `/desloppify/engine/_plan/persistence.py` uses a **graceful degradation pattern**: - Line 26-36: `load_plan()` catches `(json.JSONDecodeError, UnicodeDecodeError, OSError)` and returns `empty_plan()` with a warning - Line 51-55: `validate_plan()` catches `ValueError`, returns `empty_plan()` with a warning This is intentional and correct — there's **no `PLAN_LOAD_EXCEPTIONS` tuple** because the function handles errors internally. **All 27+ callsites of `load_plan()` don't need exception handling** — they receive a valid (possibly empty) plan. **Recommendation:** - Replace `raise SystemExit(string_msg)` in dev_cmd.py with proper error logging - For other SystemExit cases, create a `CommandError(Exception)` base class for user-facing errors vs. critical early-exit scenarios - Plan loading is correctly designed; no change needed there --- ### 2. **`do_run_batches()` God Function Analysis** **Location:** `/desloppify/app/commands/review/batches.py`, function `do_run_batches()` starting at line 299 **Line Count:** 581 lines (299-880, file is 880 total) **Distinct Phases Identified:** 1. **Configuration Parsing** (lines 325-370): Extracts 15+ CLI arguments, coerces them to proper types 2. **Packet Loading** (lines 371-395): Loads investigation batches and validates them 3. **Orchestration Setup** […]

Let me compile the comprehensive findings into a clear report: --- ## Comprehensive Codebase Exploration Report I've thoroughly examined the three areas you requested. Here's what I found: --- ### 1. THE `DESLOPPIFY MOVE` TOOL CAPABILITIES **Location:** `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/move/` #### Directory Structure: - `move.py` — Main command orchestrator (entry point) - `move_directory.py` — Handles entire directory moves (packages) - `move_apply.py` — File system operations + rollback on failure - `move_planning.py` — Computing import replacements with language modules - `move_language.py` — Language detection + move module loading - `move_reporting.py` — User-facing output formatting #### Can it handle moving entire directories? **YES, fully supported.** The tool has explicit directory move logic: ```python def cmd_move(args: argparse.Namespace) -> None: if source_path.is_dir(): return _cmd_move_dir(args, source_abs) # Delegates to directory logic ``` In `move_directory.py`: - Collects all source files recursively using `collect_source_files()` for target language extensions - Builds a `DirectoryMovePlan` that maps file-by-file relocations - Applies both **internal changes** (imports within moved files) and **external changes** (imports in files outside the directory) #### How it works — Import Rewriting: 1. **Language-Specific Modules**: Each language plugin has a `move.py` that implements: - `find_replacements(source, dest, graph)` → find all import statements to rewrite - `find_self_replacements(source, dest, graph)` → rewrite internal imports within the moved file - Optional: `filter_intra_package_importer_changes()` and `filter_directory_self_changes()` for directory-specific filtering - Optional: `get_verify_hint()` → suggests verification commands (e.g., `npm run type-check`) 2. **Dependency Graph**: Calls `lang.build_dep_graph(scan_path)` to construct module import relationships, then language modules use it to identify which imports must change. 3. **Replacement Strategy**: Deduplicates and groups replacements by file: - `external_changes` — changes to files outside the moved directory - `intra_package_changes` — changes within the moving directory - `self_changes` — the moved file's own internal imports 4. **Application**: Rollback-safe using atomic writes: - Moves source first via `shutil.move()` - Updates file contents with `.replace()` on identified import strings - On failure: restores original files and rolls back the move #### Argparse Definition: From `parser_groups_admin.py` (lines 78-88): ```python p_move = sub.add_parser( "move", help="Move a file or directory and update all import references" ) p_move.add_argument( "source", type=str, help="File or directory to move (relative to project root)" ) p_move.add_argument("dest", type=str, help="Destination path (file or directory)") p_move.add_argument( "--dry-run", action="store_true", help="Show changes without modifying files" ) ``` #### Supported Languages: Per the languages README, move support requires a language plugin with a `move.py` file. **Full plugins have move support** (Python, […]
